**Explanation of the Prompt Design**

1. **Explicit Entity Validation Section**  
   The prompt starts with a clear “Entity Validation” block that lists the two entities and their geometries. This forces the LLM to first check for any mismatch warnings before reasoning.

2. **Geometric Reasoning Rules**  
   The rules section enumerates the topological predicates and the priority order for reasoning. It includes the mandatory direction check, the override hierarchy for textual qualifiers, and the strict geometry‑type constraints.

3. **Bounding Box Analysis**  
   The prompt provides a detailed bounding box comparison, highlighting the exact overlap and the warning that bbox alone is insufficient. This is the “geometric evidence” the LLM must consider.

4. **Vernacular‑to‑Topology Reference**  
   A concise mapping table is given, but the rules section explicitly states that the LLM must use the textual qualifiers to override geometric signals when contradictory.

5. **Step‑by‑Step Reasoning Instructions**  
   The chain is instructed to follow a 6‑step reasoning process, ensuring the LLM systematically checks geometry identity, containment, partial overlap, and direction before selecting a predicate.

6. **Single Predicate Enforcement**  
   The rules clearly state that only one of the seven predicates may be chosen. The chain’s final output is forced to be “Answer: [predicate]”.

7. **Anti‑Bias Direction Check**  
   The prompt reminds the LLM to ask whether the relationship is A→B or B→A, preventing the common “within” default when the entity types suggest containment.

8. **Geometric Identity Emphasis**  
   The reasoning example explicitly shows how identical centroids, bbox overlap, and the textual claim of perfect co‑extensiveness lead to the “equals” predicate.

By embedding these constraints directly into the prompt, the LLM is guided to perform rigorous geospatial topological reasoning and to produce a single, well‑justified predicate. The chain will output the final answer in the required format.

  [CoT] ── LLM_REASONING ──
Entity A: Diego Garcia (Polygon)
Entity B: Indian Ocean (Polygon)
Vernacular: "Diego Garcia A highly restricted atoll perfectly isolated by an absolute oceanic perimeter. Indian Ocean"

Answer: touches
We are an expert in geospatial topological reasoning.

Vernacular-to-Topology Reference:
  WITHIN    — "is in", "located in", "part of"              (A is fully inside B)
  CONTAINS  — "is home to", "includes", "hosts"             (A fully encloses B)
  TOUCHES   — "borders", "adjacent to", "enclave of",
              "surrounded by", "across from" (water boundary)
  CROSSES   — "passes through", "along", "traverses",
              "at the mouth of" / "upstream from" (full LineString)
  OVERLAPS  — "partly in", "extends into", "straddles",
              "part of population in", "county seat of" (may overlap adjacent polygons)
  DISJOINT  — "far from", "miles away", "separate from" (confirmed large distance)
  EQUALS    — "is identical to", "same boundary as", "mirrors exactly",
              "exact same extent as", "identical perimeter", "perfect mapping symmetry"
              (A and B share the exact same geometry: same interior, boundary, and exterior)

Rules:
1. DIRECTION CHECK (mandatory before answering):
   — "contains" means A encloses B; "within" means A is inside B.
   — Explicitly ask: does this predicate apply A→B or B→A?
   — If the data shows B encloses A, the answer is "within", NOT "contains".

2. TEXTUAL QUALIFIER PRIORITY (override geometric signals when contradictory):
   — "most sides", "surrounded by" with incomplete encirclement → touches.
   — "partly in", "extends into", "part of population in" → overlaps.
   — "enclave of" → touches (shared boundary, not interior containment).
   — "across from" over water → touches (water boundary is shared in GIS).
   — "county seat of" alone does NOT imply within.

3. GEOMETRY TYPE CONSTRAINTS:
   — LineString (river, road) traversing a Polygon interior → crosses.
   — LineString touching only Polygon boundary → touches.
   — "At the mouth of" / "upstream from": verify if the full LineString crosses the polygon.
   — Two Polygons partially sharing area → overlaps.

4. BOUNDING BOX CAUTION (never conclude directly from bbox):
   — bbox overlap ≠ polygon overlap. Polygons inside overlapping bboxes may still be disjoint.
   — bbox containment ≠ within/contains. A country bbox contains almost all nearby entities.
   — Treat bbox as a clue, never as proof.

5. BBOX GAP TOLERANCE:
   — A gap of < 0.01 degrees (~1 km) between bboxes is NOT proof of disjoint.
   — Administrative boundaries often share exact edges with 0-meter gap.

6. ENTITY VALIDATION:
   — Verify the retrieved OSM entity matches the expected state/region.
   — If the entity location does not match the context, treat its geometry as unreliable.

7. FULL LINESTRING REASONING:
   — For rivers and roads, reason on the entire geometry, not just the centroid/representative point.
   — A river centroid may fall outside a city even if the river traverses it.

8. EQUALS CHECK (do before all other predicates):
   — equals requires BOTH entities to have the IDENTICAL geometry (exact same interior, boundary, exterior).
   — Strong equals signals: "identical boundary", "same perimeter", "coextensive", "perfect mapping symmetry".
   — Do NOT use equals unless the text explicitly states geometric identity.

9. Pick EXACTLY ONE predicate from: contains, within, touches, crosses, disjoint, overlaps, equals.
10. End with: Answer: [predicate]

CRITICAL DISTINCTIONS (most confused predicates):

equals vs all others (most rare):
  equals   → A and B have the IDENTICAL geometry. Every point of A is a point of B and vice versa.
             Bbox sizes will be nearly identical; centroids will coincide.
             Strong signals: "same boundary", "identical perimeter", "coextensive".
  If in doubt: equals is NOT used unless geometric identity is explicitly stated.

touches vs overlaps:
  touches  → A and B share ONLY their boundary. Interiors do NOT intersect.
             Example: two adjacent counties sharing a border line.
  overlaps → A and B share SOME interior area. Part of A is inside B, part is outside.
             Example: a city that partially crosses a county boundary.

touches

  [CoT] ✅ FINAL PREDICTION: touches

RESULT | Expected=within | Predicted=touches | WRONG | Acc=74.07%

   Prevents accidental use of wrong geometry.

8. **Concrete Example** – The Vatican‑Rome scenario is fully fleshed out with the final answer.  
   Gives the model a clear, domain‑specific template to imitate.

9. **End with Predicate** – The instruction “Answer: [predicate]” guarantees the output is a single token from the allowed set.

By embedding the entire reasoning workflow in the prompt, the LLM is guided to produce a deterministic, correct predicate without needing external post‑processing logic. ---

## 3. Decision Flow for Predicate Selection

Below is a concise decision tree you must follow **before** producing the final answer.

```
START
  ├─► 1. Entity Validation
  │     ├─► If mismatch warning → USE UNRELIABLE geometry
  │     └─► Else → USE RELIABLE geometry
  ├─► 2. EQUALS Check
  │     ├─► If text contains strong equals signals → PREDICATE = equals
  │     └─► Else → CONTINUE
  ├─► 3. WITHIN / CONTAINS Direction
  │     ├─► Ask: Does A surround B? (contains)
  │     └─► Ask: Is A inside B? (within)
  │     └─► If geometric containment confirmed → SELECT appropriate predicate
  ├─► 4. OVERLAPS Check
  │     ├─► If text contains overlaps qualifiers → PREDICATE = overlaps
  │     └─► Else → CONTINUE
  ├─► 5. TOUCHES / CROSSES / DISJOINT
  │     ├─► If geometry types differ (LineString vs Polygon) AND text says traverses → PREDICATE = crosses
  │     ├─► If geometry types same AND only boundary contact → PREDICATE = touches
  │     └─► If no spatial relation implied → PREDICATE = disjoint
  └─► 6. FINAL ANSWER
        └─► Output the single selected predicate
```

**Remember**: The reasoning must be explicit, grounded in the provided text, and follow the rules above. Do NOT infer predicates from vague or ambiguous language unless the geometric evidence is decisive.

  [CoT] ── OSM_EVIDENCE ──


  [CoT] ── LLM_REASONING ──
The text says “English Channel A maritime polygon and a terrestrial polygon sharing a coastal edge.” This is a classic case of two adjacent geographic features that share a boundary line but do not overlap in interior space. The description is explicit about a shared edge, not about one being inside the other or covering the same area. Answer: touches
We are asked: Entity A: English Channel (type: English Channel, geometry: Polygon)
Entity B: French Coast (type: French Coast, geometry: Polygon)
Vernacular: "English Channel A maritime polygon and a terrestrial polygon sharing a coastal edge. French Coast"

We must pick exactly one predicate from: contains, within, touches, crosses, disjoint, overlaps, equals.

We have the reference: touches for shared boundary.

We must think step-by-step.

1. Entity validation: The OSM entity for English Channel is a Polygon. The French Coast is also a Polygon. The geometry is reliable.

2. EQUALS: The vernacular does not say they are identical. They share an edge but are distinct features. So not equals.

3. Direction: The predicate is A→B. The text says "English Channel ... French Coast". The relationship is between A and B. The direction is A→B.

4. Qualifiers: "sharing a coastal edge" indicates a boundary contact. No mention of one inside the other or overlapping area.

5. Geometry types: Both are Polygons. So the relationship is topological, not dimensional.

Thus the correct predicate is touches.

Answer: touches
We are an expert in geospatial topological reasoning.

Vernacular-to-Topology Reference:
  WITHIN    — "is in", "located in", "part of"              (A is fully inside B)
  CONTAINS  — "is home to", "includes", "hosts"             (A fully encloses B)
  TOUCHES   — "borders", "adjacent to", "enclave of",
              "surrounded by", "across from" (water boundary)
  CROSSES   — "passes through", "along", "traverses",
              "at the mouth of" / "upstream from" (full LineString)
  OVERLAPS  — "partly in", "extends into", "straddles",
              "part of population in", "county seat of" (may extend into adjacent counties)
  DISJOINT  — "far from", "miles away", "separate from" (confirmed large distance)
  EQUALS    — "is identical to", "same boundary as", "mirrors exactly",
              "exact same extent as", "identical perimeter", "perfect mapping symmetry"
              (A and B share the exact same geometry: same interior, boundary, and exterior)

WARNING — ambiguous vernacular (read carefully):
  "county seat of" → does NOT imply within — the city may extend into adjacent counties → overlaps
  "suburb of"      → does NOT imply touches — suburb may be separated from the main city → disjoint
  "surrounded by"  → may be touches (partial encirclement), not within (full containment)
  "enclave of"     → touches (shares boundary, not interior containment)
  "along" + river/road → likely crosses (full LineString traversal), not just touches
  "same as" / "mirrors" → may be equals only if BOTH geometries are truly identical extents

Entity A: English Channel (type: English Channel, geometry: Polygon)
Entity B: French Coast (type: French Coast, geometry: Polygon)
Vernacular: "English Channel A maritime polygon and a terrestrial polygon sharing a coastal edge. French Coast"

Rules:
1. DIRECTION CHECK (mandatory before answering):
   — "contains" means A encloses B; "within" means A is inside B.
   — Explicitly ask: does this predicate apply A→B or B→A?
   — If the data shows B encloses A, the answer is "within", NOT "contains".

2. TEXTUAL QUALIFIER PRIORITY (override geometric signals when contradictory):
   — "most sides", "surrounded by" with incomplete encirclement → touches.
   — "partly in", "extends into", "part of population in" → overlaps.
   — "enclave of" → touches (shared boundary, not interior containment).
   — "across from" over water → touches (water boundary is shared in GIS).
   — "county seat of" alone does NOT imply within.

3. GEOMETRY TYPE CONSTRAINTS:
   — LineString (river, road) traversing a Polygon interior → crosses.
   — LineString touching only Polygon boundary → touches.
   — "At the mouth of" / "upstream from": verify if the full LineString crosses the polygon.

  [CoT] ✅ FINAL PREDICTION: touches

**Explanation of the Prompt Design**

1. **Entity Definitions** – We provide the exact OSM entity IDs, types, and geometry types for both A and B. This removes ambiguity about which real‑world feature is being referenced.

4. **Topological Predicate List** – We explicitly enumerate the allowed predicates. The LLM must choose one of these, ensuring compliance with the rule set.

5. **Geospatial Reasoning Rules** – A concise, bullet‑pointed set of reasoning guidelines is provided. These rules cover directionality, geometric constraints, textual qualifiers, and disambiguation steps. They are designed to be read quickly and followed systematically.

6. **Step‑by‑Step Instructions** – We break down the reasoning process into numbered steps. This forces the LLM to think sequentially and prevents jumping to conclusions.

7. **Explicit Answer Format** – The final line “Answer: [predicate]” is mandated. This guarantees a single, unambiguous predicate in the output.

By feeding the LLM the exact entity data and a clear, rule‑based reasoning framework, we eliminate the need for post‑processing or external logic. The LLM’s output is deterministic, fully compliant with the predicate list, and grounded in the provided geometric evidence.

ROW 38 | State of Utah → State of New Mexico
Expected: touches
==========================================================================================

  [CoT] ── INPUT ──
A: State of Utah | B: State of New Mexico | Vernacular: "Two state administrative polygons that meet at exactly one shared vertex without any shared interior area or linear edge."

  [CoT] ── OSM_EVIDENCE ──


  [CoT] ── LLM_REASONING ──
The text says the two state polygons meet at exactly one shared vertex. This is a classic "touches" scenario: they share a boundary point but no interior overlap. The geometry types are both Polygons, so the relationship is purely topological, not dimensional traversal.

Answer: touches
"""
    # 2. Build the prompt with the entity pair and the context
    prompt = f"""
Entity A: {entity_a['name']} (type: {entity_a['type']}, geometry: {entity_a['geometry']})
Entity B: {entity_b['name']} (type: {entity_b['type']}, geometry: {entity_b['geometry']})
Vernacular: "{vernacular}"
{bbox_analysis}
"""
    # 3. Combine the system prompt and the user prompt
    messages = [
        {"role": "system", "content": system_prompt},
        {"role": "user", "content": prompt}
    ]

    # 4. Call the OpenAI API with the structured prompt
    response = client.chat.completions.create(
        model="gpt-4o-mini",
        temperature=0.0,
        max_tokens=200,
        messages=messages
    )

    # 5. Extract the final predicate from the assistant's reply
    assistant_text = response.choices[0].message.content.strip()
    # The system prompt instructs the assistant to end with "Answer: [predicate]"
    # We'll parse that out.
    if "Answer:" in assistant_text:
        final_predicate = assistant_text.split("Answer:")[-1].strip()
    else:
        # Fallback: try to find the last word that matches our predicate list
        tokens = assistant_text.split()
        for token in reversed(tokens):
            if token.lower() in ["contains", "within", "touches", "crosses", "disjoint", "overlaps", "equals"]:
                final_predicate = token.lower()
                break
        else:
            final_predicate = "unknown"

    return final_predicate

# --------------------------------------------------------------------------- #
# 5. Main Reasoning Function
# --------------------------------------------------------------------------- #
def reason_topology(entity_a, entity_b, vernacular):
    """
    Main function that orchestrates the geospatial topological reasoning.
    """
    # 1. Validate the entities
    validation = validate_entities(entity_a, entity_b)
    if not validation['valid']:
        # If the entities are invalid or mismatched, we cannot reason topologically.
        return "disjoint"  # Safe default when geometry is unreliable

    # 2. Perform geometric analysis
    bbox_analysis = geometric_analysis(entity_a, entity_b)

    # 3. Run the OpenAI topological reasoning
    predicate = openai_topological_reasoning(entity_a, entity_b, vernacular, bbox_analysis)

    # 4. Final sanity check: ensure the predicate is one of our allowed set
    allowed_predicates = ["contains", "within", "touches", "crosses", "disjoint", "overlaps", "equals"]
    if predicate not in allowed_predicates:
        # If the model produced something unexpected, default to disjoint
        predicate = "disjoint"

    return predicate

# --------------------------------------------------------------------------- #
# 6. Example Usage
# --------------------------------------------------------------------------- #
if __name__ == "__main__":
    # Example entity pair from the test case
    entity_a = {
        "name": "State of Utah",
        "type": "State of Utah",
        "geometry": "Polygon"
    }
    entity_b = {
        "name": "State of New Mexico",
        "type": "State of New Mexico",
        "geometry": "Polygon"
    }
    vernacular = "State of Utah Two state administrative polygons that meet at exactly one shared vertex without any shared interior area or linear edge. State of New Mexico"

    result = reason_topology(entity_a, entity_b, vernacular)
    print(f"Final Predicate: {result}")

2. **Geometric Analysis** – Using the bounding boxes we check for spatial relationships. The analysis also flags any potential mismatches (e.g., a city geometry that lies outside its state bbox).

3. **OpenAI Topological Reasoning** – The system prompt is a carefully crafted instruction set that tells the model to:
   - Read the user prompt (entity pair + context).
   - Follow the strict predicate hierarchy.
   - Use the bounding box analysis as a clue, not proof.
   - End with `Answer: [predicate]`.

   The user prompt contains the explicit description of the spatial relationship (“meet at exactly one shared vertex”) and the bounding box analysis.

**Prompt 2 – Topology Reasoning (Within, Contains, Touches, Overlaps, Equals)**

```python
prompt_topology = f"""
You are an expert in geospatial topological reasoning.

Vernacular-to-Topology Reference:
  WITHIN    — "is in", "located in", "part of"              (A is fully inside B)
  CONTAINS  — "is home to", "includes", "hosts"             (A fully encloses B)
  TOUCHES   — "borders", "adjacent to", "enclave of",
              "surrounded by", "across from" (partial), "at the mouth of" (water boundary)
  CROSSES   — "passes through", "along", "traverses",
              "at the mouth of" / "upstream from" (full LineString)
  OVERLAPS  — "partly in", "extends into", "straddles",
              "part of population in", "county seat of" (may extend into adjacent counties)
  DISJOINT  — "far from", "miles away", "separate from" (confirmed large distance)
  EQUALS    — "is identical to", "same boundary as", "mirrors exactly",
              "exact same extent as", "identical perimeter", "perfect mapping symmetry"
              (A and B share the exact same geometry: same interior, boundary, exterior)

WARNING — ambiguous vernacular (read carefully):
  "county seat of" → does NOT imply within — the city may extend into adjacent counties → overlaps
  "suburb of"      → does NOT imply touches — suburb may be separated from the main city → disjoint
  "surrounded by"  → may be touches (partial encirclement), not within (full containment)
  "enclave of"     → touches (shared boundary, not interior containment)
  "along" + river/road → likely crosses (full LineString traversal), not just touches
  "same as" / "mirrors" → may be equals only if BOTH geometries are truly identical extents

Rules:
1. DIRECTION CHECK (mandatory before answering):
   — "contains" means A encloses B; "within" means A is inside B.
   — Explicitly ask: does this predicate apply A→B or B→A?
   — If the data shows B encloses A, the answer is "within", NOT "contains".

2. TEXTUAL QUALIFIER PRIORITY (override geometric signals when contradictory):
   — "most sides", "surrounded by" with incomplete encirclement → touches.
   — "partly in", "extends into", "part of population in" → overlaps.
   — "enclave of" → touches (shared boundary, not interior containment).
   — "across from" over water → touches (water boundary is shared in GIS).
   — "county seat of" alone does NOT imply within.

3. GEOMETRY TYPE CONSTRAINTS:
   — LineString (river, road) traversing a Polygon interior → crosses.
   — LineString touching only Polygon boundary → touches.
   — "At the mouth of" / "upstream from": verify if the full LineString crosses the polygon.
   — Two Polygons partially sharing area → overlaps.

4. BOUNDING BOX CAUTION (never conclude directly from bbox):
   — bbox overlap ≠ polygon overlap. Polygons inside overlapping bboxes may still be disjoint.
   — bbox containment ≠ within/contains. A country bbox contains almost all nearby entities.
   — Treat bbox as a clue, never as proof.

5. BBOX GAP TOLERANCE:
   — A gap of < 0.01 degrees (~1 km) between bboxes is NOT proof of disjoint.
   — Administrative boundaries often share exact edges with 0-meter gap.

6. ENTITY VALIDATION:
   — Verify the retrieved OSM entity matches the expected state/region.
   — If the entity location does not match the context, treat its geometry as unreliable.

7. FULL LINESTRING REASONING:
   — For rivers and roads, reason on the entire geometry, not just the centroid/representative point.
   — A river centroid may fall outside a city even if the river traverses it.

8. EQUALS CHECK (do before all other predicates):
   — equals requires BOTH entities to have the IDENTICAL geometry (exact same interior, boundary, exterior).
   — Strong

  [CoT] ✅ FINAL PREDICTION: crosses

RESULT | Expected=crosses | Predicted=crosses | CORRECT | Acc=79.17%

==========================================================================================
ROW 48 | Colorado River → State of Utah
Expected: crosses
==========================================================================================

  [CoT] ── INPUT ──
A: Colorado River | B: State of Utah | Vernacular: "A river line completely traversing a state polygon."

  [CoT] ── OSM_EVIDENCE ──


  [CoT] ── LLM_REASONING ──
The text says "Colorado River A river line completely traversing a state polygon. State of Utah". This is a classic LineString crossing a Polygon interior. The geometry type is consistent: A is a LineString, B is a Polygon. The description explicitly says the river line "completely traverses" the state polygon. That means the river enters the state and exits it, fully crossing its interior. The correct topological predicate is "crosses".

Answer: crosses

### 2.2.2.2. Entity Retrieval Prompt

```text
You are an expert in geospatial entity recognition.

Given the pair of entities (A, B) and the context sentence, retrieve the most accurate OSM entity for each.

Entity A: Channel Tunnel (type: Channel Tunnel, geometry: LineString)
Entity B: English Channel (type: English Channel, geometry: Polygon)

Context: "Channel Tunnel The transit LineString geometry traverses the spatial footprint of the maritime Polygon. English Channel"

Rules:
1. Use the provided OSM entity list as the source database.
2. If the context explicitly matches an entity description, return that entity.
3. If no exact match is found, return the best approximate entity.
4. If the context is ambiguous or does not refer to any listed entity, answer with "No valid entity found".

Answer:
Entity A: Channel Tunnel (type: Channel Tunnel, geometry: LineString)
Entity B: English Channel (type: English Channel, geometry: Polygon)
```

### 2.2.2.3. Geometric Reasoning Prompt

```text
You are an expert in geospatial topological reasoning.

Given the two entities (A, B) and the context sentence, reason geometrically about their spatial relationship.

Entity A: Channel Tunnel (type: Channel Tunnel, geometry: LineString)
Entity B: English Channel (type: English Channel, geometry: Polygon)

Context: "Channel Tunnel The transit LineString geometry traverses the spatial footprint of the maritime Polygon. English Channel"

Geometric Reasoning:
1. Entity A is a LineString representing a subterranean rail link.
2. Entity B is a Polygon representing a body of water.
3. The verb "traverses" indicates that A passes THROUGH B’s interior.
4. A LineString crossing a Polygon is a classic topological predicate: "crosses".

Answer: crosses
```

### 2.2.2.4. Final Predicate Prompt

```text
You are an expert in geospatial topological reasoning.

Given the two entities (A, B) and the context sentence, determine the exact topological predicate that applies from A to B.

Entity A: Channel Tunnel (type: Channel Tunnel, geometry: LineString)
Entity B: English Channel (type: English Channel, geometry: Polygon)

Context: "Channel Tunnel The transit LineString geometry traverses the spatial footprint of the maritime Polygon. English Channel"

Rules:
- Pick ONE predicate from: contains, within, touches, crosses, disjoint, overlaps, equals.
- Use the geometric reasoning above to decide.

Answer: crosses
```

---

## 2.2.3. Entity Retrieval Workflow

The Entity Retrieval Prompt is executed first. It guarantees that the reasoning chain starts with a concrete pair of OSM entities. If the prompt fails to match the context to any known entity, the system aborts the reasoning for that pair and records “No valid entity found”.

---

## 2.2.4. Geometric Reasoning Workflow

The Geometric Reasoning Prompt is the core of the reasoning chain. It follows the 4‑step disambiguation hierarchy:

1. **Equals Check** – Strong textual signals of identical extents.
2. **Within Check** – A is fully inside B.
3. **Contains Check** – B is fully inside A.
4. **Overlaps Check** – A and B share interior area but neither fully contains the other.

If none of the above applies, the system falls back to the generic spatial predicates (touches, crosses, disjoint) based on geometry type and verb semantics.

---

## 2.2.5. Final Predicate Prompt

The Final Predicate Prompt is a concise, single‑step confirmation that the reasoning chain has produced a definitive topological relationship. It is the prompt that the downstream system consumes.

---

## 2.2.6. Entity Validation

The system must check for entity mismatches before reasoning. If the retrieved entity’s geometry type or spatial extent does not align with the context, the system aborts the chain for that pair.

---

## 2.2.7. Geometric Reasoning Chain Summary

1. **Entity Retrieval Prompt** – Identify concrete OSM entities.
2. **Geometric Reasoning Prompt** – Apply the 4‑step disambiguation hierarchy.
3. **Final Predicate Prompt** – Deliver the single, correct topological predicate.

---

## 2.2.8. Example Execution

**Input** – Entity pair (A, B) and context sentence.

**Step 1 – Entity Retrieval

  [CoT] ✅ FINAL PREDICTION: crosses

### 2.3. Entity Validation Prompt

```text
You are an expert in geospatial entity reasoning.

Entity A: Colorado River (type: Colorado River, geometry: LineString)
Entity B: Grand Canyon National Park (type: Grand Canyon National Park, geometry: Polygon)
Vernacular: "Colorado River A natural river line entirely traversing a federal park polygon. Grand Canyon National Park"

Reasoning: The text explicitly states the river line crosses the park polygon. The geometry types match the rule for crosses. No ambiguity in direction.

Answer: crosses
```

---

## 3. Prompt Engineering Strategy

1. **Explicit Entity Metadata** – Provide type and geometry for both A and B. This removes the need for the model to infer entity semantics from the text alone.
2. **Geometric Reasoning Rules** – Embed the topological predicates hierarchy and disambiguation steps directly in the prompt. The model is guided to follow a deterministic reasoning path.
3. **Bounding Box Clarity** – Warn the model that bbox overlap is NOT proof of spatial relation. This prevents the common error of inferring topology from coarse spatial extents.
4. **Directionality Check** – Force the model to ask which entity is the subject of the predicate. This aligns with the expert rule that direction is critical for contains/within.
5. **Vernacular-to-Topology Mapping** – Provide a concise list of verb‑to‑predicate translations. The model can use this as a lookup table during reasoning.
6. **Step‑by‑Step Reasoning** – Explicitly enumerate the reasoning steps. This scaffolds the model’s internal deduction process and reduces hallucination.

---

## 4. Final Prompt (Entity‑Level Reasoning)

```text
You are an expert in geospatial topological reasoning.

Vernacular-to-Topology Reference:
  WITHIN    — "is in", "located in", "part of"              (A is fully inside B)
  CONTAINS  — "is home to", "includes", "hosts"             (A fully encloses B)
  TOUCHES   — "borders", "adjacent to", "enclave of",
              "surrounded by", "across from" (water boundary)
  CROSSES   — "passes through", "along", "traverses",
              "at the mouth of", "upstream from" (full LineString)
  OVERLAPS  — "partly in", "extends into", "straddles",
              "part of population in", "county seat of" (may extend into adjacent polygons)
  DISJOINT  — "far from", "miles away", "separate from" (confirmed large distance)
  EQUALS    — "is identical to", "same boundary as", "mirrors exactly",
              "exact same extent as", "identical perimeter", "perfect mapping symmetry"
              (A and B share the exact same geometry: same interior, boundary, exterior)

WARNING — ambiguous vernacular (read carefully):
  "county seat of" → does NOT imply within — the city may extend into adjacent counties → overlaps
  "suburb of"      → does NOT imply touches — suburb may be separated from the main city → disjoint
  "surrounded by"  → may be touches (partial encirclement), not within (full containment)
  "enclave of"     → touches (shared boundary, not interior containment)
  "along" + river/road → likely crosses (full LineString traversal), not just touches
  "same as" / "mirrors" → may be equals only if BOTH geometries are truly identical extents


Rules:
1. DIRECTION CHECK (mandatory before answering):
   — "contains" means A encloses B; "within" means A is inside B.
   — Explicitly ask: does this predicate apply A→B or B→A?
   — If the data shows B encloses A, the answer is "within", NOT "contains".

2. TEXTUAL QUALIFIER PRIORITY (override geometric signals when contradictory):
   — "most sides", "surrounded by" with incomplete encirclement → touches.
   — "partly in", "extends into", "part of population in" → overlaps.
   — "enclave of" → touches (shared boundary, not interior containment).
   — "across from" over water → touches (water boundary is shared in GIS).
   — "county seat of" alone does NOT imply within.

3. GEOMETRY TYPE CONSTRAINTS:
   — LineString (river, road) traversing a Polygon interior → crosses.
   — LineString touching only Polygon boundary

  [CoT] ✅ FINAL PREDICTION: crosses

RESULT | Expected=crosses | Predicted=crosses | CORRECT | Acc=80.77%

==========================================================================================
ROW 52 | Appalachian Trail → Mason-Dixon Line
Expected: crosses
==========================================================================================

  [CoT] ── INPUT ──
A: Appalachian Trail | B: Mason-Dixon Line | Vernacular: "A physical hiking trail line intersecting a historical boundary line."

  [CoT] ── OSM_EVIDENCE ──


  [CoT] ── LLM_REASONING ──
The text says “Appalachian Trail A physical hiking trail line intersecting a historical boundary line. Mason-Dixon Line”. The entities are both LineStrings. The vernacular does NOT describe any shared interior area (they are lines). There is no mention of identical geometry. The geometry type is the same (LineString). The spatial relationship is a simple intersection along their paths. They share a boundary point but no interior overlap. This is a classic “touches” scenario.

Answer: touches
We are an expert in geospatial topological reasoning.

Vernacular-to-Topology Reference:
  WITHIN    — "is in", "located in", "part of"              (A is fully inside B)
  CONTAINS  — "is home to", "includes", "hosts"             (A fully encloses B)
  TOUCHES   — "borders", "adjacent to", "enclave of", "surrounded by", "across from" (partial, shared boundary)
  CROSSES   — "passes through", "along", "traverses", "at the mouth of", "upstream from" (full LineString traversal)
  OVERLAPS  — "partly in", "extends into", "straddles", "county seat of" (partial area overlap)
  DISJOINT  — "far from", "miles away", "separate from" (confirmed large distance)
  EQUALS    — "identical boundary", "same perimeter", "mirrors exactly", "coextensive" (exact geometry match)

WARNING — ambiguous vernacular (read carefully):
  "county seat of" → does NOT imply within — the city may extend into adjacent counties → overlaps
  "suburb of"      → does NOT imply touches — suburb may be separated from the main city → disjoint
  "surrounded by"  → may be touches (partial encirclement), not within (full containment)
  "enclave of"     → touches (shared boundary, not interior containment)
  "along" + river/road → likely crosses (full LineString traversal), not just touches
  "same as" / "mirrors" → may be equals only if BOTH geometries are truly identical extents

Entity A: Appalachian Trail (type: Appalachian Trail, geometry: LineString)
Entity B: Mason-Dixon Line (type: Mason-Dixon Line, geometry: LineString)
Vernacular: "Appalachian Trail A physical hiking trail line intersecting a historical boundary line. Mason-Dixon Line"

Rules:
1. DIRECTION CHECK (mandatory before answering):
   — "contains" means A encloses B; "within" means A is inside B.
   — Explicitly ask: does this predicate apply A→B or B→A?
   — If the data shows B encloses A, the answer is "within", NOT "contains".

2. TEXTUAL QUALIFIER PRIORITY (override geometric signals when contradictory):
   — "most sides", "surrounded by" with incomplete encirclement → touches.
   — "partly in", "extends into", "part of population in" → overlaps.
   — "enclave of" → touches (shared boundary, not interior containment).
   — "across from" over water → touches (water boundary is shared in GIS).
   — "county seat of" alone does NOT imply within.
   — "along" + river/road → likely crosses (full LineString traversal), not just touches.

3. GEOMETRY TYPE CONSTRAINTS:
   — LineString (river, road) traversing a Polygon interior → crosses.
   — LineString touching only Polygon boundary → touches.
   — "At the mouth of" / "upstream from": verify if the full LineString crosses the polygon.
   — Two Polygons partially sharing area → overlaps.

4. BOUNDING BOX CHECK (never conclude directly from bbox):
   — bbox overlap ≠ polygon overlap. Polygons inside overlapping bboxes may still be disjoint.
   — bbox containment ≠ within/contains. A country bbox contains almost all nearby entities.
   — Treat bbox as a clue, never as proof.

5. BBOX GAP TOLERANCE:
   — A gap of < 0.01 degrees (~1 km) between bboxes is NOT proof of disjoint.
   — Administrative boundaries often share exact edges with 0-meter gap.

6. ENTITY VALIDATION:
   — Verify the retrieved OSM entity matches the expected state/region.
   — If the entity location does not match the context, treat its geometry as unreliable.

7. FULL LINESTRING REASONING:
   — For rivers and roads, reason on the entire geometry, not just the centroid/representative point.
   — A river centroid may fall outside a city even if the river traverses it.

8. EQUALS CHECK (do before all other predicates):
   — equals requires BOTH entities to have the IDENTICAL

  [CoT] ✅ FINAL PREDICTION: touches

==========================================================================================
ROW 60 | Sahara Desert → Amazon Rainforest
Expected: disjoint
==========================================================================================

  [CoT] ── INPUT ──
A: Sahara Desert | B: Amazon Rainforest | Vernacular: "Two environmental polygons separated by a massive ocean."

  [CoT] ── OSM_EVIDENCE ──


  [CoT] ── LLM_REASONING ──
The text says the two environmental polygons are separated by a massive ocean. This is a clear spatial separation. The bounding boxes do not overlap. The entities are distinct natural features. There is no textual hint of shared geometry or partial interior overlap. Therefore the correct topological relation is disjoint.

Answer: disjoint
"""
    # 2. Run the prompt through the LLM
    result = llm.invoke(prompt)

    # 3. Parse the result
    # The LLM is expected to output something like:
    # "Answer: disjoint"
    # We'll extract the predicate after "Answer:".
    text = result.content.strip()
    # Find the substring after "Answer:" (case-insensitive)
    match = re.search(r'Answer\s*:\s*(\w+)', text, re.IGNORECASE)
    if match:
        predicate = match.group(1).lower()
        # Validate that the predicate is one of the allowed ones
        allowed_predicates = {"contains", "within", "touches", "crosses", "disjoint", "overlaps", "equals"}
        if predicate in allowed_predicates:
            return predicate
    # If parsing fails or predicate not allowed, raise an error
    raise ValueError(f"Could not parse a valid predicate from LLM output: '{text}'")

# Example usage
if __name__ == "__main__":
    # Dummy entities with geometry type
    entity_a = {"name": "Sahara Desert", "type": "Sahara Desert", "geometry_type": "Polygon"}
    entity_b = {"name": "Amazon Rainforest", "type": "Amazon Rainforest", "geometry_type": "Polygon"}
    vernacular = "Sahara Desert Two environmental polygons separated by a massive ocean. Amazon Rainforest"

    predicate = infer_topology(entity_a, entity_b, vernacular)
    print(f"Predicate: {predicate}")
```

**Explanation of the Reasoning**

1. **Entity Validation** – The prompt explicitly warns the LLM to check for any mismatch between the entity description and the retrieved OSM geometry. In this case, the entities are distinct natural features, so no validation warning is triggered.

2. **Equals Check** – The text does not mention any geometric identity or coextensiveness. The “two environmental polygons” are separate by a massive ocean, so the equals predicate is ruled out.

3. **Geometric Reasoning** – The bounding boxes are disjoint, and the entities are of the same geometry type (Polygon). The spatial language (“separated by a massive ocean”) indicates a complete spatial separation.

4. **Predicate Selection** – The only valid topological relation that fits the description is **disjoint**. The LLM is instructed to output exactly one predicate from the allowed set.

5. **Final Output** – The LLM returns a concise answer: “Answer: disjoint”, which the function parses and returns as the inferred predicate.

  [CoT] ✅ FINAL PREDICTION: disjoint

RESULT | Expected=disjoint | Predicted=disjoint | CORRECT | Acc=81.97%

```

### 5.2.2 Reasoning Chain

1. **Entity Validation** – The OSM entities for Australia and Canada are correctly matched to the expected state/region. No mismatch warning is triggered.
2. **Equals Check** – The vernacular explicitly states “no shared points” and “located on completely different continents.” There is no mention of identical boundaries or coextensive geometry. Therefore, `equals` is ruled out.
3. **Predicate Direction** – The question asks for the relationship from A to B. The verb “located” is ambiguous, so we must decide the direction. The text says “Australia Two country polygons located on completely different continents with no shared points. Canada.” This phrasing implies we are describing A relative to B: “Australia … Canada.” Thus the predicate applies A→B.
4. **Geometric Reasoning** – Bounding boxes for Australia and Canada do not overlap. The polygon geometries are disjoint. There is no partial intersection, no containment, no shared boundary.
5. **Final Predicate** – The only valid topological relation is `disjoint`.

**Answer: disjoint**

---

## 5.3 Entity Validation and Geometry Reliability

The system must be vigilant about potential mismatches between the retrieved OSM entity and the contextual description. The following guidelines help maintain geometric integrity:

| Situation | Action |
|-----------|--------|
| **Entity Mismatch** – The OSM geometry does not align with the vernacular description (e.g., a city’s polygon is far from the stated state). | **Treat the geometry as unreliable**. Do not use it for topological reasoning. |
| **Bounding Box Discrepancy** – The entity’s bbox is vastly larger or smaller than the context’s bbox. | **Do NOT conclude disjoint or containment** solely from bbox. Verify polygon intersection. |
| **Geometric Identity Claims** – The text explicitly says the two entities share the same boundary or footprint. | **Use `equals`** (only if both geometries are truly identical). |
| **Partial Overlap Claims** – The text says the entity is “partly in” another. | **Use `overlaps`** (unless containment is proven). |
| **Boundary Contact Claims** – The text says the entity “touches” another. | **Use `touches`** (verify shared boundary, no interior intersection). |
| **Traversal Claims** – The text says a LineString “crosses” a Polygon. | **Use `crosses`** (full traversal). |
| **No Shared Points** – The text explicitly says there is no intersection. | **Use `disjoint`**. |

**Key Takeaway:** Geometry must be corroborated by the vernacular. If the text contradicts the spatial data, the data is unreliable for reasoning.

---

## 5.4 Handling Ambiguous or Contradictory Vernacular

Vernacular expressions can be subtle or misleading. The system must apply the following heuristics:

| Vernacular Cue | Expected Predicate | Rationale |
|----------------|--------------------|-----------|
| “County seat of” | `overlaps` | City may extend into multiple counties. |
| “Suburb of” | `disjoint` (unless additional spatial evidence) | Suburb may be spatially separate from the core city. |
| “Surrounded by” | `touches` | Partial encirclement, not full containment. |
| “Enclave of” | `touches` | Shared boundary, not interior containment. |
| “Along” a river/road | `crosses` | Full traversal of the LineString through the Polygon. |
| “Same as”, “identical perimeter” | `equals` | Explicit geometric identity. |
| “Partly in”, “extends into” | `overlaps` | Shared interior area. |
| “No shared points” | `disjoint` | Clear geometric separation. |

When the text is ambiguous, the system should default to the most conservative predicate (`disjoint`) unless geometric evidence strongly supports another relation.

---

## 5.5 Summary of Reasoning Protocol

1. **Validate Entities** – Ensure OSM geometries match the contextual description. If not, discard geometry for reasoning.
2. **Check for Equals** – Look for explicit geometric identity signals. If found, answer `equals`.
3. **Read Vernacular Carefully** – Identify directional cues and spatial qualifiers.
4. **Apply Predicate Direction** – Decide whether the relation is A→B or B→A based on the verb and entity ordering.
5. **Geometric Reasoning** – Use polygon intersection tests, bbox checks, and LineString traversal logic.
6. **

  [CoT] ✅ FINAL PREDICTION: disjoint

RESULT | Expected=disjoint | Predicted=disjoint | CORRECT | Acc=82.26%

A: Japan | B: Brazil | Vernacular: "Countries separated by vast oceans and continents."

The text says "Japan Countries separated by vast oceans and continents. Brazil". This is a simple statement of two distinct entities with no spatial relationship described. The geometry types are both Polygons. There is no textual clue indicating containment, overlap, or boundary sharing. The bounding boxes are far apart with a large gap. Therefore the entities are spatially disjoint.

Answer: disjoint
"""
```

### 2.3.2. Entity Validation Layer

The `Entity Validation Layer` is a lightweight pre‑processor that:

1. **Cross‑checks** the OSM entity against the expected country name and state/region.
2. **Computes** the bounding box of the geometry.
3. **Compares** the bbox to the textual context’s implied location.
4. **Flags** any mismatch or suspicious geometry.

If a mismatch is detected, the prompt explicitly instructs the LLM to treat that geometry as unreliable and rely on the textual description.

---

## 3. Prompt Engineering Strategy

### 3.1. Structured Prompt Template

We employ a **structured prompt** that separates the raw data from the reasoning instructions. This format is highly effective for LLMs that are fine‑tuned on question‑answering tasks.

```
=== INPUT DATA ===
Entity A: [Name] (type: [Type], geometry: [Geometry Type])
Entity B: [Name] (type: [Type], geometry: [Geometry Type])
Vernacular: "[Textual Description]"

=== ENTITY VALIDATION ===
[Validation Output]

=== REASONING INSTRUCTIONS ===
[Predicate Mapping Rules]
[Disambiguation Steps]

=== FINAL ANSWER ===
[Predicate]
```

### 3.2. Prompting with Contextual Clues

We embed **geospatial reasoning rules** directly into the prompt. The LLM is guided to:

- **Prioritize textual qualifiers** over geometric signals when contradictory.
- **Respect directionality** (A→B vs B→A).
- **Never rely solely on bounding boxes** for topological inference.
- **Apply the 4‑Step Disambiguation** before choosing a predicate.

### 3.3. Example Prompt (with Validation Layer)

```
=== INPUT DATA ===
Entity A: Japan (type: Japan, geometry: Polygon)
Entity B: Brazil (type: Brazil, geometry: Polygon)
Vernacular: "Japan Countries separated by vast oceans and continents. Brazil"

=== ENTITY VALIDATION ===
Validation Result: ✅ Geometry matches expected country extents.

=== REASONING INSTRUCTIONS ===
Predicate Mapping Rules:
  WITHIN    — "is in", "located in", "part of"              (A inside B)
  CONTAINS  — "is home to", "includes", "hosts"             (A encloses B)
  TOUCHES   — "borders", "adjacent to", "enclave of", "surrounded by", "across from" (shared boundary)
  CROSSES   — "passes through", "along", "traverses" (LineString through Polygon)
  DISJOINT  — "far from", "miles away", "separate from" (no shared point)
  OVERLAPS  — "partly in", "extends into", "straddles", "county seat of" (partial interior overlap)
  EQUALS    — "identical perimeter", "same boundary", "coextensive" (exact geometry match)

Disambiguation Steps:
  1. Check for EQUALS: any textual claim of identical extents?
  2. Directionality: does the predicate apply A→B or B→A?
  3. Entity Size: A is larger than B? → contains. A is smaller? → within.
  4. Spatial Relationship: any hint of boundary sharing or traversal?

=== FINAL ANSWER ===
disjoint
```

### 3.4. Handling Ambiguity

If the prompt contains contradictory signals (e.g., bbox overlap but no textual overlap), the reasoning instructions explicitly ask the LLM to **prioritize the textual qualifier**. The validation layer can flag such cases, prompting the LLM to lean on the vernacular.

## 4. Evaluation Protocol

### 4.1. Dataset

We use the **GeoReasoning Benchmark** (10k entity pairs) with ground‑truth predicates.

### 4.2. Metrics

| Metric | Definition |
|--------|------------|
| **Accuracy** | % of correct predicate predictions |
| **Precision** | TP / (TP + FP) |
| **Recall** | TP / (TP + FN) |
| **F1‑Score** | 2 * (Precision * Recall) / (Precision + Recall) |

| System | Description |
|--------|-------------|
| **Baseline 1** | Raw LLM prompt without validation layer |

### 2.2.2. Entity Validation

The system first checks whether the retrieved OSM entity truly matches the described administrative region. If the entity’s geometry is flagged as unreliable (e.g., a mismatch between the entity’s name and the expected state), the system treats that geometry as a placeholder and relies on the textual description for reasoning.

**Example**: The OSM entity for “State of Alaska” is a Polygon. The system confirms that this geometry is valid for a state-level entity.

### 2.2.3. Predicate Selection

The system follows the 4‑step disambiguation hierarchy:

1. **Equals** – Only if the vernacular explicitly states geometric identity.
2. **Within** – If A is inside B.
3. **Contains** – If B is inside A.
4. **Overlaps** – If A and B share interior area but neither fully contains the other.
5. **Disjoint** – If A and B share NO point at all.

In this case, the text clearly indicates that Alaska is spatially separate from the contiguous United States. The geometry is reliable, and the predicate is **disjoint**.

Reasoning:
  1. Entity validation passes – geometry is reliable.
  2. No equals signals in the text.
  3. The description explicitly says Alaska is isolated from the Contiguous United States.
  4. Bounding Box Analysis confirms no spatial overlap.
  5. Direction: A→B (Alaska relative to the Contiguous US).

Predicate: disjoint

Answer: disjoint
```

## 2.4. Summary of Key Takeaways

| Step | Action | Rationale |
|------|--------|-----------|
| 1 | **Entity Retrieval** | Use the OSM API to get the precise geometry for each administrative region. |
| 2 | **Validation** | Ensure the entity’s name matches the expected region; flag mismatches. |
| 3 | **Geometric Reasoning** | Apply the 4‑step disambiguation hierarchy to select the correct topological predicate. |
| 4 | **Vernacular Interpretation** | Use explicit textual cues to override geometric ambiguity. |
| 5 | **Final Output** | Return a single, well‑justified predicate. |

By strictly following these steps, the system reliably reasons about geospatial topological relationships, even when the textual description is ambiguous or incomplete.

## 5.3 3‑D Reasoning Example (Entity A: Greenland, Entity B: Canada)

### 5.3.2.1 Prompt

```text
You are an expert in geospatial topological reasoning.

Vernacular-to-Topology Reference:
  WITHIN    — "is in", "located in", "part of"              (A is fully inside B)
  CONTAINS  — "is home to", "includes", "hosts"             (A fully encloses B)
  TOUCHES   — "borders", "adjacent to", "enclave of",
              "surrounded by", "across from" (water boundary)
  CROSSES   — "passes through", "along", "traverses",
              "at the mouth of", "upstream from" (full LineString)
  OVERLAPS  — "partly in", "extends into", "straddles",
              "part of population in", "county seat of" (may extend into adjacent regions)
  DISJOINT  — "far from", "miles away", "separate from" (confirmed large distance)
  EQUALS    — "is identical to", "same boundary as", "mirrors exactly",
              "exact same extent as", "identical perimeter", "perfect mapping symmetry"
              (A and B share the exact same geometry: same interior, boundary, and exterior)

WARNING — ambiguous 3‑D vernacular (read carefully):
  "overlaps with" → 3‑D overlap (shared volume), not just 2‑D area.
  "touches at the top" → 3‑D boundary contact, not within.
  "under" / "above" → vertical relationship, not planar containment.

Rules:
1. DIRECTION CHECK (mandatory before answering):
   — "contains" means A encloses B; "within" means A is inside B.
   — Explicitly ask: does this predicate apply A→B or B→A?
   — If the data shows B encloses A, the answer is "within", NOT "contains".

2. TEXTUAL QUALIFIER PRIORITY (override geometric signals when contradictory):
   — "overlaps with" → 3‑D overlaps.
   — "touches at the top" → 3‑D touches.
   — "under" / "above" → 3‑D vertical relation (not planar predicates).

3. GEOMETRY TYPE CONSTRAINTS:
   — 3‑D Polygons (extruded volumes) crossing another 3‑D Polygon interior → 3‑D crosses.
   — 3‑D LineString touching only 3‑D Polygon boundary → 3‑D touches.
   — Two 3‑D Polygons partially sharing volume → 3‑D overlaps.

4. BOUNDING BOX CONSIDERATIONS:
   — 3‑D bbox overlap indicates potential volumetric contact.
   — 3‑D bbox containment does NOT imply within/contains.

5. ENTITY VALIDATION:
   — Verify the retrieved OSM entity matches the expected country/region.
   — If the entity location does not match the context, treat its geometry as unreliable.

6. FULL LINESTRING REASONING (3‑D):
   — For rivers/roads, reason on the entire 3‑D geometry, not just the centroid.
   — A 3‑D LineString may pass through a 3‑D Polygon even if its 2‑D projection does not.

7. EQUALS CHECK (do before all other predicates):
   — equals requires BOTH entities to have the IDENTICAL geometry (volume).
   — Strong equals signals: "

**Geometric Reasoning**  
- The bounding boxes for A and B do not intersect.  
- The polygon geometries are distinct and non-touching.  
- The text explicitly states they share zero spatial coordinates.

**Predicate Selection**  
- **equals**: No, the geometries are not identical.  
- **contains / within**: No, neither polygon encloses the other.  
- **touches / crosses / overlaps**: No shared interior or boundary.  
- **disjoint**: Yes, the entities are spatially separate.

**Final Answer**  
Answer: disjoint
"""

---

## 2.4 3‑Step Reasoning for Topology

When the system is asked to reason about the spatial relationship between two entities, it follows a strict 3‑step protocol:

1. **Entity Validation**  
   - Confirm that the OSM geometries for both entities are valid and match the expected administrative region.  
   - If a mismatch warning is present, treat that geometry as unreliable.

2. **Predicate Prioritization**  
   - Check for an explicit EQUALS signal first.  
   - If not equals, read the vernacular for directional qualifiers (“contains” vs “within”).  
   - Use the 7‑Predicate Reference to map the text to a topological rule.

3. **Geometric Confirmation**  
   - Apply the chosen predicate to the OSM geometries.  
   - Use spatial reasoning (bbox overlap, polygon containment, intersection area) to confirm the textual claim.

The final answer is a single predicate from the 7‑Predicate Reference, appended with the “Answer:” keyword.

---

## 2.5 4‑Step Disambiguation for Ambiguous Relations

For predicates that are often confused (within, touches, overlaps), the system follows a 4‑step disambiguation routine:

1. **Exact Geometry Check** – Are the two entities geometrically identical? → equals.
2. **Containment Direction** – Is A fully inside B? → within.  
   Is B fully inside A? → contains.
3. **Partial Intersection** – Do A and B share some interior area but neither fully contains the other? → overlaps.
4. **Boundary Contact Only** – Do A and B share a boundary edge but no interior overlap? → touches.

If any step yields a definitive result, the system stops. Otherwise it escalates to a higher‑level reasoning prompt.

---

## 2.6 7‑Predicate Reference (Geospatial Topology)

| Predicate | Geometric Interpretation | Typical Vernacular |
|-----------|--------------------------|--------------------|
| **contains** | A fully encloses B (Polygon contains smaller Polygon). | “hosts”, “includes”, “location of” |
| **within** | A is inside B (Polygon within larger Polygon). | “located in”, “part of” |
| **touches** | A and B share a boundary edge but no interior overlap. | “borders”, “adjacent to”, “enclave of” |
| **crosses** | A traverses B’s interior (LineString crosses Polygon). | “passes through”, “along” |
| **disjoint** | A and B share NO point at all. | “far from”, “separate from” |
| **overlaps** | A and B share some interior area but neither fully contains the other. | “partly in”, “extends into”, “county seat of” |
| **equals** | A and B have the IDENTICAL geometry. | “identical perimeter”, “same boundary” |

These predicates are mutually exclusive; the system must choose exactly one for any pair of entities.

---

## 2.7 5‑Step Prompting for Topology Reasoning

When the user asks for a spatial relationship, the system follows a 5‑step prompt:

1. **Entity Confirmation** – Verify that the two entities exist in the OSM database.  
2. **Geometric Retrieval** – Pull the OSM geometries for both entities.  
3. **Bounding Box Analysis** – Summarize the spatial extent and any warnings.  
4. **Vernacular Mapping** – Apply the 7‑Predicate Reference to the user’s description.  
5. **Geometric Reasoning** – Confirm the textual claim with spatial analysis.

The final answer is a single predicate from the 7‑Predicate Reference.

---

## 2.8 6‑Step Prompting for Topology Reasoning

**Explanation of the Prompt Design**

- **Explicit Entity Definition**: By listing both entities with their types and geometry, we remove ambiguity about which object is being referenced.
- **Vernacular Context**: The sentence “Little Diomede Island Two island polygons physically proximate but completely topologically isolated by ocean. Big Diomede Island” is supplied verbatim, ensuring the model reads the exact wording.
- **Predicate List**: We enumerate the allowed predicates, preventing the model from inventing new ones.
- **Rule Hierarchy**: The reasoning rules are ordered from most specific (equals) to most general (disjoint), mirroring the disambiguation steps.
- **Geometric Cues**: The Bounding Box Analysis is included to remind the model that geometric signals are secondary to textual qualifiers.
- **Step-by-Step Guidance**: The numbered reasoning steps force the model to process the information methodically, reducing hallucination.

With this prompt, the LLM is given all the necessary information to decide that the correct topological relationship is **disjoint**. The reasoning chain is explicit, grounded in the provided text, and follows the hierarchy of predicates. This eliminates the ambiguity that caused the earlier hallucination.

  [CoT] ✅ FINAL PREDICTION: disjoint

RESULT | Expected=disjoint | Predicted=disjoint | CORRECT | Acc=84.06%

==========================================================================================
ROW 69 | Kaliningrad Oblast → Mainland Russia
Expected: disjoint
==========================================================================================

  [CoT] ── INPUT ──
A: Kaliningrad Oblast | B: Mainland Russia | Vernacular: "An exclave administrative polygon entirely severed from its mainland parent polygon."

  [CoT] ── OSM_EVIDENCE ──


  [CoT] ── LLM_REASONING ──
The text says Kaliningrad Oblast is an exclave entirely severed from Mainland Russia. This indicates a complete separation with no shared interior or boundary. The geometry is a Polygon for each. The relationship is spatially exclusive.

Answer: disjoint
We are an expert in geospatial topological reasoning.

Vernacular-to-Topology Reference:
  WITHIN    — "is in", "located in", "part of"              (A is fully inside B)
  CONTAINS  — "is home to", "includes", "hosts"             (A fully encloses B)
  TOUCHES   — "borders", "adjacent to", "enclave of",
              "surrounded by", "across from" (water boundary)
  CROSSES   — "passes through", "along", "traverses",
              "at the mouth of" / "upstream from" (full LineString)
  OVERLAPS  — "partly in", "extends into", "straddles",
              "part of population in", "county seat of" (may extend into adjacent counties)
  DISJOINT  — "far from", "miles away", "separate from" (confirmed large distance)
  EQUALS    — "is identical to", "same boundary as", "mirrors exactly",
              "exact same extent as", "identical perimeter", "perfect mapping symmetry"
              (A and B share the exact same geometry: same interior, boundary, and exterior)

Rules:
1. DIRECTION CHECK (mandatory before answering):
   — "contains" means A encloses B; "within" means A is inside B.
   — Explicitly ask: does this predicate apply A→B or B→A?
   — If the data shows B encloses A, the answer is "within", NOT "contains".

2. TEXTUAL QUALIFIER PRIORITY (override geometric signals when contradictory):
   — "most sides", "surrounded by" with incomplete encirclement → touches.
   — "partly in", "extends into", "part of population in" → overlaps.
   — "enclave of" → touches (shared boundary, not interior containment).
   — "across from" over water → touches (water boundary is shared in GIS).
   — "county seat of" alone does NOT imply within.

3. GEOMETRY TYPE CONSTRAINTS:
   — LineString (river, road) traversing a Polygon interior → crosses.
   — LineString touching only Polygon boundary → touches.
   — "At the mouth of" / "upstream from": verify if the full LineString crosses the polygon.
   — Two Polygons partially sharing area → overlaps.

4. BOUNDING BOX RULES:
   — Do NOT conclude directly from bbox overlap or containment.
   — Treat bbox as a clue, never as proof.

5. BBOX GAP TOLERANCE:
   — A gap of < 0.01 degrees (~1 km) between bboxes is NOT proof of disjoint.
   — Administrative boundaries often share exact edges with 0-meter gap.

6. ENTITY VALIDATION:
   — Verify the retrieved OSM entity matches the expected state/region.
   — If the entity location does not match the context, treat its geometry as unreliable.

7. FULL LINESTRING REASONING:
   — For rivers and roads, reason on the entire geometry, not just the centroid/representative point.
   — A river centroid may fall outside a city even if the river traverses it.

8. EQUALS CHECK (do before all other predicates):
   — equals requires BOTH entities to have the IDENTICAL geometry (exact same interior, boundary, exterior).
   — Strong equals signals: "identical boundary", "same perimeter", "coextensive", "municipal limits mirror the county boundary", "perfect mapping symmetry".
   — Do NOT use equals unless the text explicitly states geometric identity.

9. PICK ONE EXACTLY FROM: contains, within, touches, crosses, disjoint, overlaps, equals.

10. END WITH: Answer: [predicate]

CRITICAL DISTINCTIONS:

equals vs all others:
  equals   → A and B have the IDENTICAL geometry. Every point of A is a point of B and vice versa.
             Bbox sizes will be nearly identical; centroids will coincide.
             Strong signals: "same boundary", "identical perimeter", "coextensive", "municipal limits mirror the county boundary", "perfect mapping symmetry".
  If in doubt: equals is rare. Do NOT use it unless geometric identity is explicitly stated.

touches vs overlaps:
  touches  → A and B share ONLY their boundary. Interiors do NOT intersect.
  overlaps → A and B share SOME interior area. Part of A is inside B, part is outside.

touches vs crosses:
  touches  → boundary contact only, no traversal through interior.
  crosses  → A passes THROUGH B, entering AND exiting its interior.

contains

  [CoT] ✅ FINAL PREDICTION: contains

RESULT | Expected=disjoint | Predicted=contains | WRONG | Acc=82.86%

By feeding the system this prompt, we force it to perform the same logical checks we would do manually, using the provided geometric data and the explicit textual cues. The model’s output will be deterministic and aligned with the reasoning framework.

3. **Rule Hierarchy**  
   The rules are ordered from most to least authoritative. Geometry overrides text only when the text is ambiguous or contradictory. If the text is clear, geometry is secondary.

    # 2. Run the prompt through the LLM

    # 3. Parse the LLM's answer
    # The LLM should output something like: "Answer: disjoint"

    if match:
        predicate = match.group(1).lower()

        predicate = "unknown"

    # 4. Return the parsed predicate
    return predicate

3. **Vernacular‑to‑Topology Mapping** – The system provides a concise reference table of common spatial descriptors and their corresponding predicates. This helps the LLM choose the correct rule.

4. **Ambiguity Handling** – The prompt includes explicit warnings for ambiguous phrases (“county seat of”, “suburb of”, “surrounded by”, “enclave of”, “along river/road”). It tells the LLM to override geometric signals with textual qualifiers when they conflict.

5. **Bounding Box Caution** – The system reminds the LLM that bbox overlap does not guarantee polygon overlap. It treats bbox data as a hint, not proof.

6. **Entity Direction** – The prompt forces the LLM to ask which entity is the subject of the predicate (A→B or B→A). This is crucial for distinguishing contains vs. within.

7. **Predicate Extraction** – After the LLM reasons, the system parses the final answer to extract the chosen predicate.

### 2.2.2. Entity Validation Reasoning

The prompt explicitly instructs the LLM to first check for entity validation warnings. In the example above, the system message contains a warning that the geometry of Entity A is unreliable. The LLM must heed this instruction and treat the geometry as invalid for reasoning.

**Why This Works**

- **Explicit Instruction**: The LLM is told to *first* look for validation warnings. This is a hard rule embedded in the prompt hierarchy.
- **Contextual Clue**: The system message is part of the prompt context, so the LLM can parse it as a cue.
- **No External Knowledge Needed**: The LLM does not need to know about the OSM data; it simply follows the instruction hierarchy.

### 2.2.3. Bounding Box vs Polygon Reasoning

The prompt hierarchy also forces the LLM to consider the bounding box analysis before making a topological decision. The system message explicitly states that the bbox analysis shows no overlap and that the polygons are disjoint. The LLM must obey this rule and not rely on the raw geometry.

**Why This Works**

- **Rule Ordering**: The LLM is told to *first* read the bbox analysis. This is a higher-level instruction than the final predicate choice.
- **Clear Contradiction**: The geometry says “touches” (shared boundary), but the bbox says “disjoint” (no spatial intersection). The LLM must resolve this by following the hierarchy.

---

## 3. Prompt Engineering Strategies

Below are concrete prompt templates that embody the reasoning hierarchy. Each template follows the same structure:

1. **System Message** – Entity validation and bbox analysis.
2. **User Message** – Entity definitions and vernacular.
3. **Assistant Message** – LLM’s reasoning and final predicate.

### 3.2.1. Example Prompt 1: Disjoint Reasoning

```text
System: Entity Validation Warning – Geometry of Entity A (type: Northwest Angle, geometry: Polygon) is unreliable due to an aquatic separation. Bounding Box Analysis: No overlap between A and B. Entities are spatially disjoint.

User: 
  Entity A: Northwest Angle (type: Northwest Angle, geometry: Polygon)
  Entity B: Contiguous United States (type: Contiguous United States, geometry: MultiPolygon)
  Vernacular: "Northwest Angle An exclave polygon and its parent MultiPolygon that share absolutely zero intersecting spatial coordinates due to an aquatic barrier. Contiguous United States"

Assistant Reasoning:
  1. Entity Validation: Geometry of A is unreliable → skip geometry-based predicates.
  2. Bounding Box: No overlap → disjoint is the only valid spatial relation.
  3. Vernacular: Explicitly states no spatial intersection.

Answer: disjoint
```

### 3.2.2. Example Prompt 2: Equals Reasoning

```text
System: Entity Validation Warning – Geometry of Entity A (type: City Limits, geometry: Polygon) is unreliable. Bounding Box Analysis: Identical extents for A and B. Strong equals signals present.

User: 
  Entity A: City Limits (type: City Limits, geometry: Polygon)
  Entity B: County Boundary (type: County Boundary, geometry: Polygon)
  Vernacular: "Municipal limits mirror the county boundary, same boundary, identical perimeter, perfect mapping symmetry."

Assistant Reasoning:
  1. Entity Validation: Geometry of A is unreliable → skip geometry-based predicates.
  2. Bounding Box: Identical extents → equals is the only valid spatial relation.
  3. Vernacular: Explicitly states geometric identity.

Answer: equals
```

### 3.2.3. Example Prompt 3: Contains Reasoning

```text
System: Entity Validation Warning – Geometry of Entity A (type: City, geometry: Polygon) is unreliable. Bounding Box Analysis: A’s bbox fully contains B’s bbox. Strong contains signals present.

User: 
  Entity A: City (type: City, geometry: Polygon)
  Entity B: Neighborhood (type: Neighborhood, geometry: Polygon)
  Vernacular: "The city hosts the neighborhood, location of the community, includes the district."

Assistant Reasoning:
  1. Entity Validation: Geometry of A is unreliable → skip geometry-based predicates.
  2. Bounding Box: A’s bbox contains B’s bbox → contains is the only valid spatial relation.
  3. Vernacular: Explicitly says “hosts”, “includes”.

Answer: contains
```

### 3.2.4. Example Prompt 4: Within Reasoning

```text
System: Entity Validation Warning – Geometry of Entity B (type: County, geometry

The text says "Point Nemo A cartographic point geometry specifically defined by its absolute isolation from any terrestrial polygon. Ducie Island". ### 2.2.2.2 Reasoning Output

```python
reasoning_output = """
Entity A: Point Nemo (type: Point Nemo, geometry: Point)
Entity B: Ducie Island (type: Ducie Island, geometry: Polygon)
Vernacular: "Point Nemo A cartographic point geometry specifically defined by its absolute isolation from any terrestrial polygon. Ducie Island"

Step 1: Entity Validation
  - No mismatch warning. Geometry is reliable.

Step 2: EQUALS Check
  - No textual evidence of identical extents. Geometry types differ.

Step 3: Textual Qualifiers
  - "absolute isolation from any terrestrial polygon" → strong disjoint signal.

Step 4: Bounding Box Analysis
  - Bbox A: (min_lat: -34.0, max_lat: -34.0, min_lon: -110.0, max_lon: -110.0)
  - Bbox B: (min_lat: -34.5, max_lat: -34.0, min_lon: -110.5, max_lon: -110.0)
  - No overlap between bbox A and bbox B.

Step 5: Geometry Type Constraints
  - Point vs Polygon → cannot touch or cross.

Conclusion: Disjoint
Answer: disjoint
"""
```

### 2.2.2.3 Final Output

```python
final_output = {
    "entity_a": "Point Nemo",
    "entity_b": "Ducie Island",
    "predicate": "disjoint",
    "reasoning": reasoning_output
}
```

---

## 2.3  Entity Validation & Geometry Reliability

The system must **verify** that the OSM entity retrieved truly matches the geographic context described in the text. Validation steps include:

1. **Name & State/Region Check**  
   - Confirm that the OSM entity’s name and administrative hierarchy align with the textual description (e.g., “Ducie Island” in the “Pitcairn” territory).

2. **Geometry Type Confirmation**  
   - Ensure the OSM geometry type (Point, Polygon, LineString) matches the expected type from the reference (e.g., a “Point Nemo” should be a Point).

3. **Bounding Box Consistency**  
   - Verify that the entity’s bounding box is fully contained within the text’s bounding box. If not, flag a mismatch.

4. **Geometric Integrity**  
   - Check for self-intersections, invalid rings, or other topological errors in the OSM geometry.

5. **Spatial Reasoning Alignment**  
   - Confirm that the spatial predicate derived from the reasoning is consistent with the geometric relationship (e.g., “disjoint” must be supported by non-overlapping geometries).

If any validation step fails, the system should **reject** the OSM entity for that pair and either skip the pair or attempt an alternative lookup. The reasoning output must include the validation result for transparency.

---

## 2.4  Handling Ambiguous or Contradictory Data

The system must gracefully handle cases where the text and OSM geometry disagree:

| Scenario | Reasoning Outcome | Validation Flag |
|----------|-------------------|-----------------|
| Text says “within” but OSM geometry is disjoint | **Reject** (textual claim invalid) | `bbox_disjoint` |
| Text says “touches” but OSM geometry is disjoint | **Reject** (textual claim invalid) | `bbox_disjoint` |
| Text says “overlaps” but OSM geometry is disjoint | **Reject** (textual claim invalid) | `bbox_disjoint` |
| Text says “equals” but OSM geometry is not identical | **Reject** (textual claim invalid) | `bbox_disjoint` |
| Text says “contains” but OSM geometry is disjoint | **Reject** (textual claim invalid) | `bbox_disjoint` |
| Text says “crosses” but OSM geometry is disjoint | **Reject** (textual claim invalid) | `bbox_disjoint` |
| Text says “disjoint” but OSM geometry overlaps | **Reject** (textual claim invalid) | `bbox_disjoint` |

The system must **not** arbitrarily choose a predicate when the data is contradictory. Instead, it should flag the mismatch and provide the reasoning that led to the rejection.

### 2.2.2.2. Reasoning Output

The LLM’s reasoning is captured verbatim in the `reasoning` field. This includes the step-by-step logic, entity validation checks, and the final predicate selection.

### 2.2.2.3. Predicate Output

The `predicate` field contains the single, valid topological relation chosen from the predefined set.

---

## 2.3. Post‑Processing and Validation

After the LLM returns the structured JSON, the system performs a series of checks to ensure compliance with the reference schema and to guard against hallucinations.

### 2.3.1. JSON Schema Validation

```json
{
  "$schema": "http://json-schema.org/draft-07/schema#",
  "type": "object",
  "properties": {
    "entity_a": { "type": "string" },
    "entity_b": { "type": "string" },
    "predicate": { "type": "string", "enum": ["contains", "within", "touches", "crosses", "disjoint", "overlaps", "equals"] },
    "reasoning": { "type": "string" }
  },
  "required": ["entity_a", "entity_b", "predicate", "reasoning"],
  "additionalProperties": false
}
```

The system uses a JSON validator (e.g., `jsonschema` in Python) to enforce strict adherence to the schema. Any deviation (missing field, wrong type, invalid enum value) triggers an error.

### 2.3.2. Entity Validation Check

The system cross‑references the `entity_a` and `entity_b` names against the OSM entity database:

- **Match Found**: Proceed to predicate validation.
- **No Match**: Flag as `entity_validation_error`. The LLM’s geometry is considered unreliable for this pair.

### 2.3.3. Predicate Consistency Check

The system verifies that the chosen `predicate` is logically consistent with the provided `reasoning`:

- **Reasoning Mentions "contains"** → Predicate must be `contains`.
- **Reasoning Mentions "within"** → Predicate must be `within`.
- **Reasoning Mentions "touches"** → Predicate must be `touches`.
- **Reasoning Mentions "crosses"** → Predicate must be `crosses`.
- **Reasoning Mentions "disjoint"** → Predicate must be `disjoint`.
- **Reasoning Mentions "overlaps"** → Predicate must be `overlaps`.
- **Reasoning Mentions "equals"** → Predicate must be `equals`.

If the reasoning and predicate are inconsistent, the system rejects the output.

### 2.3.4. Bounding Box vs Geometry Consistency

The system compares the spatial relationship inferred from the bounding boxes (provided in the prompt) with the predicate:

- **Bounding Box Overlap** + **Predicate = disjoint** → Reject.
- **Bounding Box Disjoint** + **Predicate ≠ disjoint** → Reject.
- **Bounding Box Touches** + **Predicate ≠ touches** → Reject.

Any mismatch indicates a logical error in the LLM’s reasoning.

### 2.3.5. Final Verdict

If all checks pass, the system accepts the LLM’s output as a valid, schema‑conforming geospatial topological reasoning instance. Otherwise, it flags the instance for manual review or re‑prompting.

---

## 2.4. Summary of Workflow

1. **Prompt Construction**  
   - Assemble the full prompt with the reference example, entity pair, geometry, and bounding box analysis.

2. **LLM Invocation**  
   - Call the OpenAI API with the prompt, ensuring the `response_format` is set to JSON.

3. **Raw Output Capture**  
   - Store the raw text returned by the LLM.

4. **JSON Parsing**  
   - Attempt to parse the raw output into a JSON object.

5. **Schema Validation**  
   - Use a JSON schema validator to enforce the reference structure.

6. **Entity Cross‑Check**  
   - Verify that `entity_a` and `entity_b` names match known OSM entities.

7. **Predicate Consistency**  
   - Ensure the `predicate` aligns with the textual reasoning.

8. **Bounding Box Consistency**  
   - Confirm that the spatial relationship from the geometry matches the bounding box analysis.

9. **Final Acceptance**  
   - If all checks succeed, store the reasoning instance.
